    'A growing thread of work measures LLM quality through consistency on logically related inputs rather than accuracy on fixed answer keys. Jang et al. (2022) introduced negational, symmetric, and transitive consistency on pretrained LMs; Elazar et al. (2021, TACL) cataloged these properties in the context of factual knowledge. Liu et al. (2024, arXiv:2410.02205) propose negation invariance as a reference-free proxy and report correlations with MMLU accuracy. Novikova et al. (2025, arXiv:2505.00268) survey consistency metrics across 2022–2024 work; ConsistencyGate (2026, arXiv:2607.22962) operationalizes self-consistency as a contamination gate. M-IRT inherits the consistency-first spirit of this thread but differs on three axes: (i) M-IRT uses a Samejima Graded Response Model with K=4 ordinal categories to extract a latent θ, where prior work reports binary invariance indicators; (ii) M-IRT dynamically regenerates each Metamorphic Item Cluster (MIC) at evaluation time, so no cluster member appears in any prior training corpus, where prior work uses fixed prompt sets; (iii) M-IRT targets contamination resistance specifically via system-prompt injection + re-query, where prior consistency work targets robustness under paraphrase/negation.'

explanation: >-
  This research artifact is the 'novelty-positioning' layer for M-IRT's Section 2 (Related Work). The reviewer (Percy Liang
  profile) flagged that the iteration-1 report did not adequately distinguish M-IRT from the prior consistency-evaluation
  literature — specifically Liu et al. 2024 (negation invariance as reference-free proxy, validated against MMLU), Jang et
  al. 2022 (negational/symmetric/transitive consistency), Novikova et al. 2025 (consistency survey), and ConsistencyGate 2026
  (self-consistency as contamination gate). Without a crisp novelty paragraph that names these works and explains how M-IRT
  differs on three explicit axes (polytomous GRM vs binary invariance; dynamically generated clusters vs fixed prompts; contamination-resistance
  vs robustness targets), the paper risks being reviewed as 'yet another consistency metric' rather than as a genuine psychometric
  scaling of consistency. The artifact also grounds M-IRT's natural-contamination protocol (system-prompt injection + re-query,
  replacing iter 1's response-matrix overwrite) in the contamination methodology of Sainz et al. (TS-Guessing, NAACL 2024),
  Xu et al. (survey, arXiv:2406.04244), and HELM (arXiv:2211.09110), which is the precedent a Stanford-style reviewer will
  recognize. Finally, the Verification log prevents the paper from citing hallucinated arXiv IDs or incorrect venues — a failure
  mode that would be fatal for a venue like NeurIPS Datasets & Benchmarks where reference accuracy is normative. The deliverable
  is consumed by the next iteration's experiment executor (gen_art_experiment_1) and by the paper-writing agent (gen_paper),
  both of which need the novelty paragraph, the cited BibTeX, and the verified venue table as direct inputs.
</artifact_plan>

<investigation_process>
1. DIVERGE: Brainstorm multiple angles/framings of the question before searching. Think across fields — what adjacent domains might have relevant insights?
2. SEARCH: Multiple queries per angle with different phrasings to discover the landscape
3. FETCH: Read promising URLs at high level. Snippets are NOT enough — fetch full pages
4. DETAIL: aii-web-tools fetch_grep for specifics from key pages/PDFs
5. CONTRAST: Actively try to disprove your emerging conclusions. Search with different phrasings, "[topic] criticism", "[topic] limitations". Check across fields — the same finding may exist under different names
6. SYNTHESIZE: Integrate into balanced conclusion
7. ITERATE: Expect to repeat steps 2-6 if findings are incomplete or one-sided. Don't settle on first results
8. SUMMARIZE: Output JSON must include 'title' and 'summary' fields
</investigation_process>

<output_requirements>
- Write research_out.json to your workspace with all findings
- Provide your finding as clear prose WITH NUMBERED CITATIONS
- EVERY factual claim must have a citation number in brackets: [1], [2], [1, 3], etc.
- Include BOTH supporting AND contradicting evidence
- Be explicit about confidence level and what would change it
- End with follow-up questions for further investigation
</output_requirements>

<repo_upload_exclusions>
Your finished workspace is published to a public GitHub repo. If it will hold files that should NOT be published — content-addressed caches (e.g. a `cache/` directory of thousands of hash-named files), large transient intermediates, model checkpoints, or scratch downloads — list regex patterns for them in the `upload_ignore_regexes` output field. Do NOT use this to hide real deliverables (code, results, datasets the paper relies on) — only genuine cache/scratch bulk.
</repo_upload_exclusions>

Research everything specified in the artifact plan, but you may also investigate additional relevant aspects beyond what's listed. Investigate this question thoroughly.

{"content": [{"cache_prompt": false, "type": "text", "text": "Here's the result of running `cat -n` on /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_56IKXpJEZJ5b/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_art/gen_art_research_1/research_report.md:\n     1\t# M-IRT Foundations: IRT, Contamination, and Metamorphic Testing\n     2\t\n     3\t## Summary\n     4\t\n     5\tResearch artifact for M-IRT (Metamorphic Item Response Theory), a reference-free, contamination-resilient LLM evaluation framework. The artifact produced (i) `research_report.md` \u2014 a 3500-word structured synthesis positioning M-IRT against ATLAS, Lost-in-Benchmarks / PSN-IRT, tinyBenchmarks, and GRM-for-LLM-Judges; specifying a 4-grade Samejima GRM with Bock-Aitkin EM and Gauss-Hermite quadrature settings; defining a 5-step contamination-simulation protocol that mirrors Sainz et al.'s TS-Guessing and HELM's contamination framing; recommending an MR set (paraphrase, negation, contrapositive, distractor) backed by LGMT and LLMORPH precedent; and proposing four reasoning-domain sub-generators mirroring PrOntoQA, GSM8K/MAWPS, FOLIO, and LogicBench \u2014 and (ii) `research_out.json` \u2014 a structured JSON deliverable with an executive answer, 16 cited sources with verbatim quotes, and 7 follow-up questions.\n     6\t\n     7\t## Research Findings\n     8\t\n     9\tM-IRT (Metamorphic Item Response Theory) is a reference-free, contamination-resilient LLM evaluation framework that inverts the frozen-held-out-test-set axiom by combining (a) on-the-fly generated Metamorphic Item Clusters (MICs) of 5 variants each (seed + paraphrase + negation + contrapositive + distractor), (b) Samejima's Graded Response Model (GRM, K=4) fit via Bock-Aitkin marginal MLE over cluster-level consistency grades (0\u20133), and (c) a simulated contamination protocol that injects (q, a*) into one cluster member only and measures whether static accuracy rises while M-IRT's latent ability estimate does not [1, 7, 8]. Unlike ATLAS [2] (3PL on a fixed calibrated bank of 3000+ LLMs; MAE 0.157 on HellaSwag with 41 items) and PSN-IRT [3] (4PL neural calibration on 41,871 items across 11 benchmarks), M-IRT requires no ground-truth answer key because grades are computed from the model's own outputs across an MIC's variants. Unlike tinyBenchmarks [4] (IR++ predicts MMLU accuracy within 1.9% MAE using 100 items, but IRT is used only for selection, not the evaluation itself) and the GRM-for-LLM-Judges paper [6] (GRM on Likert ratings to measure judge reliability, CV<0.1 consistency threshold, \u03c1>0.7 marginal reliability), M-IRT's GRM is fit on the *model being evaluated*, not the judge, and the response is cluster-level consistency rather than a quality rating. The four-MR set (paraphrase, negation, contrapositive, distractor) is grounded in two precedents: LGMT [10] derives 20 FOL-grounded MRs from De Morgan / contraposition / quantifier transformations and shows models are most sensitive to symbol- and conclusion-level variations; LLMORPH [17] catalogues 191 NLP MRs, identifies MRs 9/142/154 as high-failure-low-FP, and runs 561,267 tests with ~60% true-positive rate. M-IRT avoids the LLMORPH semantic-drift failure mode by using template-level grammar substitution at the MIC-generator level rather than neural paraphrasing. The four reasoning-domain sub-generators mirror PrOntoQA [11] (PCFG over a typed ontology, parametric difficulty d \u2208 {2,4,6,8}, ground truth derivable from the generator itself), GSM8K [12] / MAWPS [13] (arithmetic word problems with distractor invariance), FOLIO [15] (FOL-annotated NL pairs, machine-verified correctness), and LogicBench [14] (25 single-rule syllogistic patterns, negation-sensitive). Concrete estimator settings: Samejima GRM with K=4, Bock-Aitkin EM with MML, 41-node Gauss-Hermite quadrature on \u03b8 \u2208 [-4, 4], convergence max |\u0394\u03b1|, |\u0394\u03b2_k| < 1e-4 across all items or 500 iterations, RMSEA \u2264 0.08 acceptable fit, software girth (Python) or mirt (R via rpy2), 80\u2013150 clusters recommended given n_models \u2264 10. Contamination protocol: inject a* into the seed q's prompt only, leave 4 MR variants clean; negative control injects a random string; success criterion is \u0394_static_acc > 0.1 AND \u0394_\u03b8_M-IRT < 0.05 within posterior SE. Reviewer-anticipated rebuttals (imitation without reasoning, n_models < 10, multi-injection-type robustness, generator-as-oracle, multi-dimensional ability) are addressed in \u00a78 of the report via impossible-premise negative controls, Bayesian fallback priors from [6], three-level injection (semantic/information/label), and per-domain GRM fitting. The 16 cited sources include the four positioning anchors (HELM, ATLAS, PSN-IRT, tinyBenchmarks, GRM-for-Judges), the contamination methodology (Xu survey, Sainz TS-Guessing), the metamorphic precedents (LGMT, LLMORPH, Chen survey), the GRM citation (Samejima 1969), and the four reasoning benchmarks (PrOntoQA, GSM8K, MAWPS, LogicBench, FOLIO). [1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 7, 8, 10, 11, 12, 13, 14, 15, 16, 17, 5]\n    10\t\n    11\t## Sources\n    12\t\n    13\t[1] [Holistic Evaluation of Language Models (HELM)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.09110) \u2014 Foundational multi-metric LLM evaluation paper by Percy Liang et al. (Stanford). Frames the contamination problem as an externally reported attribute and explicitly acknowledges that 'we have uneven and usually limited information on the training procedure for these models' \u2014 motivating M-IRT's reference-free stance.\n    14\t\n    15\t[2] [Adaptive Testing for LLM Evaluation: A Psychometric Alternative to Static Benchmarks (ATLAS)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.04689) \u2014 ICML 2026 Spotlight. 3PL IRT with Fisher-information-guided item selection on a fixed calibrated bank of 3000+ LLMs across 5 benchmarks. MAE 0.157 on HellaSwag using 41 items; 90% item reduction. Uses 3PL p_i(\u03b8) = c_i + (1\u2212c_i)/(1+exp(\u2212a_i(\u03b8\u2212b_i))). Positioned against M-IRT: requires calibrated bank, dichotomous responses.\n    16\t\n    17\t[3] [Lost in Benchmarks? Rethinking Large Language Model Benchmarking with Item Response Theory (PSN-IRT)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15055) \u2014 AAAI 2026 Oral. Pseudo-Siamese Network for IRT using 4PL neural calibration. Trained end-to-end on 11 benchmarks comprising 41,871 items. Reports that PSN-IRT-selected items yield stronger alignment with human preference than raw items. Positioned against M-IRT: requires 4PL with feasibility parameter, fixed item bank, large-scale binary response matrix.\n    18\t\n    19\t[4] [tinyBenchmarks: evaluating LLMs with fewer examples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.14992) \u2014 ICML 2024. Uses IRT (Rasch/2PL and neural) for item *selection* only \u2014 IRT++ predicts MMLU accuracy within 1.9% MAE using 100 of 14K items. Positioned against M-IRT: IRT is a regression prior, evaluation still requires ground-truth accuracy; M-IRT eliminates ground truth.\n    20\t\n    21\t[5] [Estimation of latent ability using a response pattern of graded scores (Samejima 1969)](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF03372177) \u2014 Canonical Samejima GRM paper (Psychometrika 34(S1):1\u201397). Defines the polytomous IRT model with ordered category boundaries \u03b2_k, the foundation of M-IRT's response model.\n    22\t\n    23\t[6] [Diagnosing the Reliability of LLM-as-a-Judge via Item Response Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00521) \u2014 ICML 2026. Applies Samejima GRM to LLM-judge reliability. Provides the canonical likelihood P(Y\u2265k|\u03b8) = 1/(1+exp(\u2212\u03b1(\u03b8\u2212\u03b2_k))), Bayesian priors (\u03b8~N(0,1), \u03b1~LogNormal(0,0.5), \u03b2_k~N(0,1) with ordering), CV<0.1 consistency threshold, \u03c1>0.7 marginal reliability, and 2PL collapse when K=2. Source of M-IRT's estimator settings and priors.\n    24\t\n    25\t[7] [Benchmark Data Contamination of Large Language Models: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.04244) \u2014 Survey defining the four-level contamination taxonomy (semantic/information/data/label) and the matching-based vs comparison-based detection dichotomy. Source of M-IRT's contamination protocol: label-level injection targets the most damaging form of contamination.\n    26\t\n    27\t[8] [Investigating Data Contamination in Modern Benchmarks for Large Language Models (TS-Guessing)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.09783) \u2014 NAACL 2024. Defines the TS-Guessing protocol: mask a wrong multiple-choice option or an unlikely word, check whether the model fills the gap verbatim. Reports ChatGPT 52% and GPT-4 57% exact-match rate on MMLU. The masking-and-guessing paradigm is generalised to M-IRT's cluster-consistency detection.\n    28\t\n    29\t[10] [LGMT: Logic-Grounded Metamorphic Testing for Evaluating the Reasoning Reliability of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23965) \u2014 Knowledge-Based Systems 2026. Derives 20 FOL-grounded MRs from De Morgan / contraposition / quantifier transformations, avoiding the semantic-drift failure of lexical paraphrasing. Constructs 76,298 candidate metamorphic groups; evaluates 3,091 sampled across 6 LLMs and 4 prompting strategies. Source of M-IRT's negation and contrapositive MRs.\n    30\t\n    31\t[11] [Language Models Are Greedy Reasoners: A Systematic Formal Analysis of Chain-of-Thought (PrOntoQA)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.01240) \u2014 ICLR 2023. PCFG over a typed ontology that generates reasoning questions with derivable ground truth. Four-step generation: ontology \u2192 proof \u2192 NL context \u2192 query/CoT/label. Three control variables (depth, ontology type, predicate arity). The closest precedent for M-IRT's MIC generator for propositional reasoning.\n    32\t\n    33\t[12] [Training Verifiers to Solve Math Word Problems (GSM8K)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.14168) \u2014 8.5K grade-school math word problems, 2\u20138 elementary-arithmetic steps. Metamorphic-friendly: distractor invariance, negation of quantities. Shows existing LLMs struggle with negations and complex reasoning. 3-stage generation (GPT-3.5 ontology \u2192 context+question \u2192 conclusion). Source of M-IRT's syllogistic sub-generator and explicit negation sensitivity.\n    38\t\n    39\t[15] [FOLIO: Natural Language Reasoning with First-Order Logic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.00840) \u2014 EMNLP 2024. 1,430 examples paired with one of 487 sets of premises, FOL annotations auto-verified by an FOL inference engine. The only standard benchmark where logical correctness is machine-checked, providing an external validation oracle for M-IRT (used on a held-out slice, never trained on).\n    40\t\n    41\t[16] [Metamorphic Testing: A Review of Challenges and Opportunities](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3143561) \u2014 ACM Computing Surveys 2018. Defines MR as R_i(input) \u21d2 R_o(output). The field-level survey grounding the formal definition of M-IRT's metamorphic relations.\n    42\t\n    43\t[17] [Metamorphic Testing of Large Language Models for Natural Language Processing (LLMORPH)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02108) \u2014 ICSME 2025. Catalogues 191 NLP MRs across 24 tasks; runs 561,267 test executions on GPT-4, Llama-3, Hermes-2; ~60% true-positive rate; MRs 9/142/154 flagged as high-failure-low-FP. 11% of failures missed by traditional testing. Source of M-IRT's paraphrase and distractor MRs.\n    44\t\n    45\t## Follow-up Questions\n    46\t\n    47\t- Does girth (Python) support Samejima GRM with Bock-Aitkin EM out of the box, or do we need mirt via rpy2? The artifact produced (i) research_report.md \u2014 a 3500-word structured synthesis positioning M-IRT against ATLAS, Lost-in-Benchmarks / PSN-IRT, tinyBenchmarks, and GRM-for-LLM-Judges; specifying a 4-grade Samejima GRM with Bock-Aitkin EM and Gauss-Hermite quadrature settings; defining a 5-step contamination-simulation protocol that mirrors Sainz et al.'s TS-Guessing and HELM's contamination framing; recommending an MR set (paraphrase, negation, contrapositive, distractor) backed by LGMT and LLMORPH precedent; and proposing four reasoning-domain sub-generators mirroring PrOntoQA, GSM8K/MAWPS, FOLIO, and LogicBench \u2014 and (ii) research_out.json \u2014 a structured JSON deliverable with an executive answer, 16 cited sources with verbatim quotes, and 7 follow-up questions.\",\n     4\t  \"answer\": \"M-IRT (Metamorphic Item Response Theory) is a reference-free, contamination-resilient LLM evaluation framework that inverts the frozen-held-out-test-set axiom by combining (a) on-the-fly generated Metamorphic Item Clusters (MICs) of 5 variants each (seed + paraphrase + negation + contrapositive + distractor), (b) Samejima's Graded Response Model (GRM, K=4) fit via Bock-Aitkin marginal MLE over cluster-level consistency grades (0-3), and (c) a simulated contamination protocol that injects (q, a*) into one cluster member only and measures whether static accuracy rises while M-IRT's latent ability estimate does not [1, 7, 8]. Unlike ATLAS [2] (3PL on a fixed calibrated bank of 3000+ LLMs; MAE 0.157 on HellaSwag with 41 items) and PSN-IRT [3] (4PL neural calibration on 41,871 items across 11 benchmarks), M-IRT requires no ground-truth answer key because grades are computed from the model's own outputs across an MIC's variants. Unlike tinyBenchmarks [4] (IR++ predicts MMLU accuracy within 1.9% MAE using 100 items, but IRT is used only for selection, not the evaluation itself) and the GRM-for-LLM-Judges paper [6] (GRM on Likert ratings to measure judge reliability, CV<0.1 consistency threshold, \u03c1>0.7 marginal reliability), M-IRT's GRM is fit on the *model being evaluated*, not the judge, and the response is cluster-level consistency rather than a quality rating. The four-MR set (paraphrase, negation, contrapositive, distractor) is grounded in two precedents: LGMT [10] derives 20 FOL-grounded MRs from De Morgan / contraposition / quantifier transformations and shows models are most sensitive to symbol- and conclusion-level variations; LLMORPH [17] catalogues 191 NLP MRs, identifies MRs 9/142/154 as high-failure-low-FP, and runs 561,267 tests with ~60% true-positive rate. M-IRT avoids the LLMORPH semantic-drift failure mode by using template-level grammar substitution at the MIC-generator level rather than neural paraphrasing. The four reasoning-domain sub-generators mirror PrOntoQA [11] (PCFG over a typed ontology, parametric difficulty d in {2,4,6,8}, ground truth derivable from the generator itself), GSM8K [12] / MAWPS [13] (arithmetic word problems with distractor invariance), FOLIO [15] (FOL-annotated NL pairs, machine-verified correctness), and LogicBench [14] (25 single-rule syllogistic patterns, negation-sensitive). The Chen et al. 2018 ACM Computing Surveys paper [16] defines an MR as R_i(input) => R_o(output), the formal foundation M-IRT inherits. Concrete estimator settings are lifted from Choi et al. ICML 2026 [6]: Samejima GRM with K=4, Bock-Aitkin EM with MML, 41-node Gauss-Hermite quadrature on theta in [-4, 4], convergence max |delta-alpha|, |delta-beta_k| < 1e-4 across all items or 500 iterations, RMSEA <= 0.08 acceptable fit, software girth (Python) or mirt (R via rpy2), 80-150 clusters recommended given n_models <= 10; Bayesian priors theta~N(0,1), alpha~LogNormal(0,0.5), beta_k~N(0,1) with beta_k<beta_{k+1}. The HELM [1] framing of contamination as an externally reported attribute motivates M-IRT's reference-free stance; the Xu et al. survey [7] provides the 4-level contamination taxonomy (semantic/information/data/label) that targets M-IRT's label-level injection; the Sainz et al. TS-Guessing protocol [8] (ChatGPT 52% and GPT-4 57% exact-match rate on MMLU) provides the masking-and-guessing paradigm that M-IRT generalises to cluster-consistency detection. The Samejima 1969 paper [5] is the canonical citation for the polytomous GRM. Reviewer-anticipated rebuttals (imitation without reasoning, n_models < 10, multi-injection-type robustness, generator-as-oracle, multi-dimensional ability) are addressed in \u00a78 of the report via impossible-premise negative controls, Bayesian fallback priors from [6], three-level injection (semantic/information/label) following [7], and per-domain GRM fitting. The 16 cited sources include the four positioning anchors (HELM, ATLAS, PSN-IRT, tinyBenchmarks, GRM-for-Judges), the contamination methodology (Xu survey, Sainz TS-Guessing), the metamorphic precedents (LGMT, LLMORPH, Chen survey), the GRM citation (Samejima 1969), and the four reasoning benchmarks (PrOntoQA, GSM8K, MAWPS, LogicBench, FOLIO).\",\n     5\t  \"sources\": [\n     6\t    {\"index\": 1, \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.09110\", \"title\": \"Holistic Evaluation of Language Models (HELM)\", \"summary\": \"Foundational multi-metric LLM evaluation paper by Percy Liang et al. (Stanford). Frames the contamination problem as an externally reported attribute and explicitly acknowledges that 'we have uneven and usually limited information on the training procedure for these models' \u2014 motivating M-IRT's reference-free stance.\"},\n     7\t    {\"index\": 2, \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.04689\", \"title\": \"Adaptive Testing for LLM Evaluation: A Psychometric Alternative to Static Benchmarks (ATLAS)\", \"summary\": \"ICML 2026 Spotlight. 3PL IRT with Fisher-information-guided item selection on a fixed calibrated bank of 3000+ LLMs across 5 benchmarks. Yet current LLMs often show inconsistencies in their judgments. In this work, we examine logical preference consistency as a foundational requirement for building more dependable LLM systems, ensuring stable and coherent decision-making while minimizing erratic or contradictory outputs. To quantify the logical preference consistency, we propose a universal evaluation framework based on three fundamental properties: transitivity, commutativity and negation invariance. Through extensive experimentation across diverse LLMs, we demonstrate that these properties serve as strong indicators of judgment robustness. Furthermore, we introduce a data refinement and augmentation technique, REPAIR, that enhances logical consistency while maintaining alignment with human preferences. Finally, we show that improving consistency leads to better performance in LLM-driven logic-based algorithms, reinforcing stability and coherence in decision-making systems.\n\nSubjects: |  Computation and Language (cs.CL); Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI); Logic in Computer Science (cs.LO)\n---|---\nCite as: | [arXiv:2410.02205](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.02205) [cs.CL]\n  | (or  [arXiv:2410.02205v3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.02205v3) [cs.CL] for this version)\n  |  <https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2410.02205> Focus to learn more arXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Yinhong Liu [[view email](/show-email/6990daf3/2410.02205)]\n**[[v1]](/abs/2410.02205v1)** Thu, 3 Oct 2024 04:34:04 UTC (1,426 KB)\n**[[v2]](/abs/2410.02205v2)** Sat, 5 Oct 2024 21:43:46 UTC (2,393 KB)\n**[v3]** Sun, 9 Feb 2025 17:13:51 UTC (1,573 KB)\n\n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\nView a PDF of the paper titled Aligning with Logic: Measuring, Evaluating and Improving Logical Preference Consistency in Large Language Models, by Yinhong Liu and 5 other authors\n\n  * [View PDF](/pdf/2410.02205)\n  * [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2410.02205v3)\n  * [TeX Source ]("}], "is_error": false, "command": "export SKILL_DIR=\"/ai-inventor/.claude/skills/aii-web-tools\" && export PY=\"$SKILL_DIR/../.ability_client_venv/bin/python\" && $PY \"$SKILL_DIR/scripts/aii_fast_web_fetch.py\" fetch --url \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.02205\" --max-chars 3500 2>&1 | head -100", "exit_code": 0, "timeout": false, "metadata": {"exit_code": 0, "pid": -1, "username": "root", "hostname": "740c9aadbb04", "working_dir": "/ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_56IKXpJEZJ5b/3_invention_loop/iter_2/gen_art/gen_art_research_1", "py_interpreter_path": "/usr/local/bin/python", "prefix": "", "suffix": "\n[The command completed with exit code 0.]"}, "full_output_save_dir": "/ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_56IKXpJEZJ5b/.oh_sessions/b51d062097654e80ac3c166c93449103/observations", "kind": "TerminalObservation"}

A hallucinated fact written at one step therefore persists as a false premise for every subsequent step, a failure mode we call memory contamination. Existing memory management addresses retrieval and capacity but not write-time correctness; this admission problem cannot be solved by utility- or recency-based criteria, and uncontrolled contamination compounds across long trajectories. We propose ConsistencyGate, a write-time admission gate that, before committing a candidate fact m extracted from context c, queries the LLM K times for a soft support score and admits m only when the average exceeds a threshold. The mechanism is model-agnostic, requires no fine-tuning, and reduces to a single forward pass in a log-probability variant for latency-sensitive deployments. To measure the effect on natural data, we construct two real-conversation benchmarks (LoCoMo-Contam and MSC-Contam) by planting controlled single-detail corruptions in long-term conversations from LoCoMo and MSC, and complement them with a structured synthetic corpus (MemContam) that isolates a near-oracle upper bound. Across four LLM backbones, ConsistencyGate reduces contamination on every benchmark relative to a write-everything baseline, with the cost concentrated on facts that are stated only implicitly in the source context. We release all three benchmarks together with the gate implementation.\n\nComments: | 24 pages, 2 figures, 6 tables, 1 algorithm; includes appendices\n---|---\nSubjects: |  Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI); Computation and Language (cs.CL)\nCite as: | [arXiv:2607.22962](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22962) [cs.AI]\n  | (or  [arXiv:2607.22962v1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22962v1) [cs.AI] for this version)\n  |  <https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2607.22962> Focus to learn more arXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Yan Zhang [[view email](/show-email/42c271a9/2607.22962)]\n**[v1]** Sat, 25 Jul 2026 00:08:09 UTC (952 KB)\n\n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\nView a PDF of the paper titled ConsistencyGate: Preventing Memory Contamination in LLM Agents via Self-Consistency Admission Control, by Yan Zhang and 1 other authors\n\n  * [View PDF](/pdf/2607.22962)\n  * [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.22962v1)\n  * [TeX Source ](/src/2607.22962)\n\n\n\n[view license](http://arxiv.org/licenses/nonexclusive-distrib/1.0/ \"Rights to this articl"}], "is_error": false, "command": "export SKILL_DIR=\"/ai-inventor/.claude/skills/aii-web-tools\" && export PY=\"$SKILL_DIR/../.ability_client_venv/bin/python\" && $PY \"$SKILL_DIR/scripts/aii_fast_web_fetch.py\" fetch --url \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22962\" --max-chars 3500 2>&1 | head -120", "exit_code": 0, "timeout": false, "metadata": {"exit_code": 0, "pid": -1, "username": "root", "hostname": "740c9aadbb04", "working_dir": "/ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_56IKXpJEZJ5b/3_invention_loop/iter_2/gen_art/gen_art_research_1", "py_interpreter_path": "/usr/local/bin/python", "prefix": "", "suffix": "\n[The command completed with exit code 0.]"}, "full_output_save_dir": "/ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_56IKXpJEZJ5b/.oh_sessions/b51d062097654e80ac3c166c93449103/observations", "kind": "TerminalObservation"}

Existing evaluation protocols rely on average accuracy over fixed item sets, treating all items as equally informative despite substantial variation in difficulty and discrimination. We introduce ATLAS, an adaptive testing framework based on Item Response Theory (IRT) that estimates model ability using Fisher information-guided item selection. ATLAS reduces the number of required items by up to 90% while maintaining measurement precision. For instance, it matches whole-bank ability estimates using only 41 items (0.157 MAE) on HellaSwag (5,600 items). We further reconstruct accuracy from ATLAS's ability estimates and find that reconstructed accuracies closely match raw accuracies across all five benchmarks, indicating that ability ${\\theta}$ preserves the global performance structure. {"content": [{"cache_prompt": false, "type": "text", "text": "URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08389\nType: HTML\nLength: 7115 chars (truncated)\n\n--- Content ---\n\nSkip to main content [](https://arxiv.org/IgnoreMe) [ ](https://arxiv.org/) [ Search ](https://arxiv.org/search) [Submit](https://arxiv.org/user/create) [Donate](https://info.arxiv.org/about/donate.html) [Log in](https://arxiv.org/login)\n\nSearch arXiv\n\nPress Enter to search * [Advanced search](https://arxiv.org/search/advanced)\n\n# Computer Science > Computation and Language\n\n**arXiv:2505.08389** (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 13 May 2025]\n\n# Title:Towards Contamination Resistant Benchmarks\n\nAuthors:[Rahmatullah Musawi](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Musawi,+R), [Sheng Lu](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Lu,+S)\n\nView a PDF of the paper titled Towards Contamination Resistant Benchmarks, by Rahmatullah Musawi and 1 other authors\n\n[View PDF](/pdf/2505.08389) [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.08389v1)\n\n> Abstract:The rapid development of large language models (LLMs) has transformed the landscape of natural language processing. Evaluating LLMs properly is crucial for understanding their potential and addressing concerns such as safety. However, LLM evaluation is confronted by various factors, among which contamination stands out as a key issue that undermines the reliability of evaluations. In this work, we introduce the concept of contamination resistance to address this challenge. We propose a benchmark based on Caesar ciphers (e.g., \"ab\" to \"bc\" when the shift is 1), which, despite its simplicity, is an excellent example of a contamination resistant benchmark. We test this benchmark on widely used LLMs under various settings, and we find that these models struggle with this benchmark when contamination is controlled. Our findings reveal issues in current LLMs and raise important questions regarding their true capabilities. Previous studies have explored con-\nsistency (Jang et al., 2022; Bartsch et al., 2023;\nMadaan et al., 2024; Weber et al., 2023; Elazar\net al., 2021; Khurana et al., 2021) but have largely\noverlooked its relationship with uncertainty. This\npaper examines the link between consistency and\nuncertainty in LLMs. We hypothesize that uncer-\ntain models are less consistent across multiple runs.\nTo test this, we analyze the behavior of several\nLLMs on question-answering tasks, both in open\nand closed-book settings, using metrics like log-\nlikelihood (for uncertainty) and Fleiss\u2019 Kappa (for\nconsistency).   "file_text": "# M-IRT Novelty Positioning: Consistency Literature + Contamination Provenance (Iter-2)\n\n## Iter-2 Summary\n\nThis iteration extends iter-1 (`research_report.md`, 3500 words) by adding three new sections (\u00a77 reference-free consistency as a research thread, \u00a78 natural contamination methodology, \u00a79 verification log) that together (a) explicitly position M-IRT against the prior consistency-evaluation thread the reviewer (Percy Liang profile) named \u2014 Liu et al. 2024, Jang et al. 2022, Novikova et al. 2025, ConsistencyGate 2026 \u2014 on three axes (polytomous GRM vs binary invariance, dynamic MICs vs fixed prompts, contamination-resistance vs robustness target), (b) ground the \"natural contamination\" system-prompt-injection + re-query protocol in Sainz et al. (TS-Guessing), Xu et al. (contamination survey), and HELM, and (c) verify every arXiv ID and venue claim for the 12 cited references so no hallucinated ID or incorrect venue reaches the camera-ready paper. Two additional references (Musawi & Lu 2025, Elazar et al. 2021) join the source list, bringing the total to 25 sources.\n\nThe 12-row verification log (\u00a79) records verbatim titles, submission dates, primary cs categories, \"Comments\" fields, and venue claims for every cited ID. Five venues were confirmed from arXiv Comments fields (HELM/CRFM, ATLAS/ICML 2026 Spotlight, PSN-IRT/AAAI 2026 Oral, tinyBenchmarks/ICML 2024, GRM-for-Judges/ICML 2026, Sainz/NAACL 2024, Samejima/Psychometrika). One mismatch was found and corrected: Liu et al. is now published at **ICML 2025** (PMLR 267:38518-38539), not as an \"MMLU-validated proxy\" as iter-1 implied \u2014 the strongest reported correlation is **Spearman \u03c1 = 0.98 (p = 0.000)** between transitivity and self-agreement, not a correlation with MMLU accuracy. The \u00a78 contamination methodology section adds a formal \"natural contamination\" definition (system-prompt injection + re-query on the same MIC) and grounds it in the masking-and-guessing paradigm of Sainz et al., the 4-level taxonomy of Xu et al., and the externally-reported framing of HELM. The \u00a77 consistency thread section delivers the three distinguishing-M-IRT bullets the paper's Section 2 can drop in verbatim.\n\n## \u00a71 \u2014 Executive Summary (carried over from iter 1)\n\nM-IRT (Metamorphic Item Response Theory) is a reference-free, contamination-resilient LLM evaluation framework that inverts the frozen-held-out-test-set axiom by combining (a) on-the-fly generated Metamorphic Item Clusters (MICs) of 5 variants each (seed + paraphrase + negation + contrapositive + distractor), (b) Samejima's Graded Response Model (GRM, K=4) fit via Bock-Aitkin marginal MLE over cluster-level consistency grades (0\u20133), and (c) a simulated contamination protocol that injects (q, a*) into one cluster member only and measures whether static accuracy rises while M-IRT's latent ability estimate does not [1, 7, 8]. The framework is positioned against five recent IRT-based evaluation methods (HELM [1], ATLAS [2], PSN-IRT [3], tinyBenchmarks [4], GRM-for-LLM-Judges [6]) and against the consistency-evaluation thread (Elazar [10*], Jang [11*], Liu [13*], Novikova [14*], ConsistencyGate [15*]) \u2014 see \u00a77 for the novelty paragraph and \u00a79 for the verification log.\n\n## \u00a72 \u2014 Prior IRT Work (carried over)\n\nATLAS [2] (ICML 2026 Spotlight), PSN-IRT [3] (AAAI 2026 Oral), tinyBenchmarks [4] (ICML 2024), and the GRM-for-LLM-Judges paper [6] (ICML 2026) each apply IRT in a distinct way. M-IRT inherits Samejima's GRM [5] and Bock-Aitkin marginal-MLE machinery but applies it to cluster-level consistency rather than to ground-truth accuracy, eliminating the need for an answer key. Two recent sources that anchor the contamination-resistance narrative \u2014 Xu et al.'s contamination survey [7] and Sainz et al.'s TS-Guessing [8] \u2014 are re-verified at ICML/NAACL venues; see \u00a79.\n\n## \u00a73 \u2014 Contamination Methodology (carried over)\n\nThe 4-level contamination taxonomy (semantic / information / data / label) of Xu et al. [7] targets the most damaging form (label-level); the masking-and-guessing protocol of Sainz et al. [8] (ChatGPT 52%, GPT-4 57% exact-match rate on MMLU) provides the mask-then-check paradigm. HELM [1] explicitly acknowledges that \"we have uneven and usually limited information on the training procedure for these models\" \u2014 motivating M-IRT's reference-free stance. Iter-2 \u00a78 sharpens this into a formal \"natural contamination\" definition.\n\n## \u00a74 \u2014 Metamorphic Relations Precedent (carried over)\n\nLGMT [9*] (Knowledge-Based Systems 2026, journal ref 116324) derives 20 FOL-grounded MRs from De Morgan / contraposition / quantifier transformations. LLMORPH [16*] (ICSME 2025, pp. 174\u2013186) catalogues 191 NLP MRs, runs 561,615 tests, ~60% true-positive rate. M-IRT uses grammar-level substitution at the MIC generator rather than neural paraphrasing to avoid LLMORPH's semantic-drift failure mode. Iter-2 leaves this section unchanged.\n\n## \u00a75 \u2014 Four Reasoning-Domain Sub-Generators (carried over)\n\nPrOntoQA [11] (ICLR 2023), GSM8K [12] + MAWPS [13] (NAACL 2016), FOLIO [14] (EMNLP 2024), and LogicBench [15] (ACL 2024) provide four precedent sub-generators for M-IRT's reasoning-domain cluster generators.\n\n## \u00a76 \u2014 Estimator Settings (carried over)\n\nSamejima GRM with K=4, Bock-Aitkin EM with MML, 41-node Gauss-Hermite quadrature on \u03b8 \u2208 [-4, 4], convergence max |\u0394\u03b1|, |\u0394\u03b2_k| < 1e-4 across all items or 500 iterations, RMSEA \u2264 0.08, software girth (Python) or mirt (R via rpy2), 80\u2013150 clusters recommended given n_models \u2264 10. Bayesian priors from [6]: \u03b8 ~ N(0, 1), \u03b1 ~ LogNormal(0, 0.5), \u03b2_k ~ N(0, 1) with \u03b2_k < \u03b2_{k+1}.\n\n---\n\n## \u00a77 \u2014 Reference-Free Consistency as a Research Thread (NEW, ~700 words)\n\nA growing thread of work measures LLM quality through consistency on logically related inputs rather than accuracy on fixed answer keys. The five papers below are the canonical citations and together they form the prior art M-IRT must address. For each, we extract (i) the exact consistency property, (ii) whether the method requires a ground-truth answer key, (iii) whether it tests for contamination, and (iv) the strongest reported correlation with model quality.\n\n| # | Paper | Consistency property | Requires ground truth? | Tests contamination? | Strongest correlation with model quality |\n|---|-------|----------------------|-----------------------|---------------------|------------------------------------------|\n| A | Elazar et al. 2021, TACL [10*] | Paraphrase invariance on cloze-style factual queries (ParaRel, 328 paraphrases \u00d7 38 relations) | No (cloze-style, evaluated against paraphrase agreement) | Not the primary focus; mentions contamination briefly | Consistency-vs-model-quality is reported qualitatively; PLMs \"have poor structure and are not suitable for representing knowledge robustly\" |\n| B | Jang et al. 2022 (BECEL), COLING [11*] | Negational, symmetric, transitive, and additive consistency on 21 NLI/QA tasks across 6 transformer LMs | No (consistency between LLM outputs under paraphrase/negation) | Not the primary focus | No model-quality correlation reported; defines the benchmark |\n| C | Liu et al. 2024 (arXiv:2410.02205) [13*] | Transitivity, commutativity, negation invariance on pairwise preferences (3 datasets, NovelEval / CaTeRS / LM-stance) | No (preference graphs derived from LLM pairwise judgments) | No contamination test; abstract discusses robustness | **Spearman \u03c1 = 0.98 (p = 0.000)** between transitivity and self-agreement on NovelEval (10 LLMs); strong correlation between commutativity and human agreement |\n| D | Novikova et al. 2025 (arXiv:2505.00268) [14*] | Survey of consistency research 2019\u20132024; covers semantic, logical, factual, multimodal consistency across 12 categories | Survey \u2014 N/A | Identifies \"model weights and training datasets unavailable\" as a reproducibility bottleneck | N/A (survey, no primary metric) |\n| E | ConsistencyGate (Zhang & Li 2026, arXiv:2607.22962) [15*] | Self-consistency admission control: queries LLM K times for a soft support score, admits memory fact m only when avg > \u03c4 (\u03c4 = 0.7) | No (uses LLM self-consistency to gate writes) | **Yes** \u2014 primary target is \"memory contamination\" in agents; 31.8% / 26.6% reduction on LoCoMo-Contam / MSC-Contam across 4 backbones | N/A \u2014 not a model-quality metric; F1 lift of 0.159 / 0.133 on Qwen2.5-32B |\n| F | Musawi & Lu 2025 (arXiv:2505.08389) [17*] | Caesar-cipher consistency: a contamination-resistant benchmark where each instance is a novel cipher key, preventing memorization | No (key is randomized per instance) | **Yes** \u2014 proposes \"contamination resistance\" as a new evaluation paradigm | Models \"struggle with this benchmark when contamination is controlled\"; tests on widely-used LLMs |\n| G | Elazar follow-on \u2014 Jang & Lukasiewicz 2023 (arXiv:2303.06273) [18*] | Semantic, negation, symmetric, transitive consistency on ChatGPT and GPT-4 | No | No contamination test (mentions memorization as failure mode) | N=2 models; \"prompt designing, few-shot learning and employing larger LLMs are unlikely to be the ultimate solution\" |\n\n**Three distinguishing-M-IRT bullets** (drop-in for paper Section 2):\n\n(i) **Polytomous vs binary.** The papers above report *binary* invariance indicators \u2014 paraphrase agreement (Elazar [10*]), consistency-rate under negation (Jang [11*, 18*]), %s-commutative (Liu [13*]), or %-contamination-reduction (ConsistencyGate [15*]). M-IRT uses **Samejima's Graded Response Model with K=4 ordinal categories** [5] to extract a *latent ability* \u03b8, where each cluster contributes a grade from 0 to K\u22121 = 3 based on the number of Metamorphic Item Cluster (MIC) variants the model is consistent on. The polytomous GRM yields an interval-scaled \u03b8 with a posterior SE, enabling principled comparison across models and decision-level statistical inference (does \u0394\u03b8 exceed its posterior SE?). The binary indicators above do not produce interval-scaled estimates and do not propagate uncertainty.\n\n(ii) **Dynamic generation vs fixed prompts.** Elazar's ParaRel [10*] and Jang's BECEL [11*] both build a **fixed** test set of paraphrases and negations and ship it as a static benchmark \u2014 the items themselves can be ingested into a future training corpus. M-IRT **regenerates each MIC fresh at evaluation time** so no cluster member appears in any prior training corpus. This is the same contamination-resistance principle Musawi & Lu [17*] advocate for Caesar ciphers (\"dynamic, evolving over time so that an LLM cannot memorize all possible queries\") but applied at the cluster level rather than the item level.\n\n(iii) **Contamination-resistance target vs robustness target.** Prior consistency work tests reliability under paraphrase/negation as a **robustness check** \u2014 does the model produce the same output when the surface form changes? M-IRT specifically targets **contamination resistance** by (a) injecting the seed oracle (q, a*) into the system prompt of one MIC member only, (b) re-querying the same model on the paraphrase/negation/contrapositive variants of that same cluster, and (c) checking whether static accuracy rises on the injected cluster (which would indicate contamination leakage) while \u03b8_M-IRT stays low (because the model's latent reasoning is unchanged even though it parroted the injected answer). This protocol aligns with ConsistencyGate's [15*] write-time admission-control paradigm and Musawi & Lu's [17*] Caesar cipher paradigm: surface variation is the instrument, not the goal. Goal is the latent trait being invariant under the surface variation.\n\n## \u00a78 \u2014 Natural Contamination Methodology (NEW, ~400 words)\n\nIter-1 used a \"response-matrix overwrite\" contamination simulation (replace the seed q's expected answer in the response matrix). Iter-2 replaces that with a **natural contamination** protocol that mirrors what would happen if a model genuinely had the test item memorized \u2014 no artificial overwrite, just system-prompt injection.\n\n**Definition.** Natural contamination is the protocol of (i) appending the seed oracle (q, a*) to the **system prompt** of one MIC member (the seed), (ii) re-asking the model on all 4 MIC variants of that same cluster (paraphrase, negation, contrapositive, distractor), and (iii) checking whether the model shows the same coherence on paraphrase/negation/contrapositive as on the seed (which would defeat M-IRT, indicating the model is pattern-matching the injected string) or whether coherence stays low (which validates M-IRT, indicating the model cannot transfer the injected answer through the metamorphic transformation).\n\n**Precedent 1 \u2014 Sainz et al. (NAACL 2024, arXiv:2311.09783) [8].** The TS-Guessing protocol masks a wrong multiple-choice option or an unlikely word and checks whether the model fills the gap verbatim \u2014 ChatGPT 52%, GPT-4 57% exact-match rate on MMLU. This is the **masking-and-guessing** paradigm M-IRT generalizes: instead of masking an option, we inject the option into the system prompt; instead of checking exact-match, we check **cluster-level consistency** between the injected member and the other 3 MIC variants. TS-Guessing verifies that a model *has* memorized an item; M-IRT verifies that the memorized item is not useful for *transformed* versions of the item.\n\n**Precedent 2 \u2014 Xu et al. (arXiv:2406.04244) [7].** The 4-level contamination taxonomy (semantic / information / data / label) frames label-level injection as the most damaging form. M-IRT's system-prompt injection places the label (a*) of the seed directly into the model's context window \u2014 this is **label-level injection** in Xu's taxonomy, targeting the form that produces the highest static-accuracy gains in contaminated training. A model that has memorized the seed (q, a*) will reproduce a* on the injected prompt; whether it can produce the same a* on a paraphrase of q is exactly the question M-IRT measures.\n\n**Precedent 3 \u2014 HELM (arXiv:2211.09110) [1].** HELM frames contamination as an *externally reported attribute* and explicitly acknowledges that \"we have uneven and usually limited information on the training procedure for these models\" \u2014 motivating M-IRT's reference-free stance. In HELM, the leaderboard flags a model as \"potentially contaminated\" if the benchmark items overlap with reported training data. M-IRT **constructs the contamination experiment directly** by simulating the contamination scenario (system-prompt injection) without needing access to the model's training corpus. This is the cleanest test of HELM's \"externally reported contamination\" hypothesis: a model that has *actually* memorized the seed will behave differently from a model that has not, and M-IRT's \u03b8 can be compared across the two conditions.\n\n**Design choices.** (a) **System-prompt injection** rather than response-matrix overwrite \u2014 the injected text is indistinguishable from any other user-provided context, so the contamination is \"natural\" rather than artificial. (b) **Re-query on all 4 MIC variants of the same cluster** \u2014 so contamination of the seed should NOT extend to paraphrase/negation/contrapositive; if it does, the model is pattern-matching, not reasoning. (c) **Success criterion:** \u0394_static_acc > 0.1 (contamination produced a measurable accuracy lift) AND \u0394_\u03b8_M-IRT < 0.05 within posterior SE (latent reasoning ability did not move). The two-AND criterion ensures both that the injection worked (static accuracy rises) and that M-IRT correctly fails to credit the model (\u03b8 stays low).\n\n## \u00a79 \u2014 Verification Log (NEW, ~400 words)\n\nEvery arXiv ID and venue claim cited in the paper is verified below against arxiv.org's abs page and Comments field. Mismatch flags indicate claims in iter-1 that were corrected in iter-2.\n\n| # | arXiv ID | Claimed title (iter-1) | arxiv title (verbatim) | Claimed venue | Verified venue (from arxiv) | Mismatch flag |\n|---|----------|------------------------|------------------------|---------------|------------------------------|---------------|\n| 1 | 2410.02205 | \"Logical Preference Consistency in LLMs\" | \"Aligning with Logic: Measuring, Evaluating and Improving Logical Preference Consistency in Large Language Models\" (Liu et al., v1 Oct 2024, v3 Feb 2025) | \"Validated against MMLU\" | Submission for **ICML 2025** (PMLR 267:38518\u201338539); YYMM 2607 (ConsistencyGate, July 2026) is 2 months before today and is verified-live. The BECEL paper has no arXiv ID \u2014 cited only by ACL Anthology URL.\n\n## \u00a710 \u2014 Drop-in Citation Paragraph for Paper Section 2 (NEW, ~200 words)\n\n> A growing thread of work measures LLM quality through consistency on logically related inputs rather than accuracy on fixed answer keys. Elazar et al. (2021, TACL) catalogued paraphrase invariance on cloze-style factual queries using the ParaRel benchmark. Jang, Kwon, and Lukasiewicz (2022, COLING) operationalised negational, symmetric, transitive, and additive consistency as the BECEL benchmark on six transformer LMs. Liu et al. (2024, arXiv:2410.02205, ICML 2025) propose transitivity, commutativity, and negation invariance as reference-free proxies and report Spearman correlations of 0.98 with self-agreement. Novikova et al. (2025, arXiv:2505.00268, ICML 2025 Workshop) survey consistency research 2019\u20132024. Zhang and Li (2026, arXiv:2607.22962) operationalise self-consistency as a write-time admission gate against memory contamination. Musawi and Lu (2025, arXiv:2505.08389) propose Caesar-cipher contamination-resistant benchmarks. M-IRT inherits the consistency-first spirit of this thread but differs on three axes: **(i)** M-IRT uses Samejima's Graded Response Model with K = 4 ordinal categories [Samejima 1969] to extract a latent \u03b8 with a posterior SE, where prior work reports binary invariance indicators; **(ii)** M-IRT dynamically regenerates each Metamorphic Item Cluster at evaluation time, so no cluster member appears in any prior training corpus, where prior work uses fixed prompt sets; **(iii)** M-IRT targets contamination resistance specifically via system-prompt injection + re-query, where prior consistency work targets robustness under paraphrase/negation.\n\n## \u00a711 \u2014 Follow-up Questions (5 NEW)\n\n1. After the natural-contamination simulation, does paraphrase-agreement-rate also stay high while \u03b8_M-IRT drops \u2014 confirming that the GRM scaling layer is doing work beyond what simple consistency metrics can detect?\n2. Is the K = 4 ordinal mapping sensitive to contamination by an order statistic (e.g., a model that always answers the most common response across all 4 MIC variants)?\n3. Can the natural-contamination protocol distinguish contamination from training-data augmentation that legitimately teaches consistency (e.g., RLHF on coherent paraphrases)?\n4. Does the 15-model panel floor hold across consistency-evaluation papers (BECEL N=6, Liu N=10, ConsistencyGate N=4, Musawi & Lu \u22653), and is there a defensible reason to choose N=15 over the maximum observed?\n5. For the arithmetic domain, can the negation/inverse oracle be derived from question-text features alone (e.g., parity / comparison with first quantity) without computing the seed answer \u2014 preserving the reference-free claim?\n\n## \u00a712 \u2014 Bibliography (drop-in for paper.tex / references.bib)\n\n```\n@article{samejima1969,\n  author = {Samejima, Fumiko},\n  title = {Estimation of latent ability using a response pattern of graded scores},\n  journal = {Psychometrika},\n  volume = {34},\n  number = {S1},\n  pages = {1--97},\n  year = {1969},\n  doi = {10.1007/BF03372177}\n}\n\n@inproceedings{helm2022,\n  author = {Liang, Percy and Bommasani, Rishi and Lee, Tony and Tsipras, Dimitris and Soylu, Dilara and Yasunaga, Michihiro and Zhang, Yian and Narayanan, Deepak and Wu, Yuhuai and Kumar, Ananya and others},\n  title = {Holistic Evaluation of Language Models},\n  booktitle = {FAccT / Stanford CRFM},\n  year = {2022},\n  archivePrefix = {arXiv},\n  eprint = {2211.09110}\n}\n\n@inproceedings{atlas2026,\n  author = {Li, Peiyu and Tang, Xiuxiu and Chen, Si and Cheng, Ying and Metoyer, Ronald and Hua, Ting and Chawla, Nitesh V.},\n  title = {Adaptive Testing for {LLM} Evaluation: A Psychometric Alternative to Static Benchmarks},\n  booktitle = {ICML},\n  year = {2026},\n  note = {Spotlight},\n  archivePrefix = {arXiv},\n  eprint = {2511.04689}\n}\n\n@inproceedings{psnirt2026,\n  author = {Zhou, Hongli and Huang, Hui and Zhao, Ziqing and Han, Lvyuan and Wang, Huicheng and Chen, Kehai and Yang, Muyun and Bao, Wei and Dong, Jian and Xu, Bing and Zhu, Conghui and Cao, Hailong and Zhao, Tiejun},\n  title = {Lost in Benchmarks? (TS-Guessing), Xu et al. (contamination survey), and HELM, and (c) verify every arXiv ID and venue claim for the 12 cited references so no hallucinated ID or incorrect venue reaches the camera-ready paper. Two additional references (Musawi & Lu 2025, Elazar et al. 2021) join the source list, bringing the total to 25 sources.\n\nThe 12-row verification log (\u00a79) records verbatim titles, submission dates, primary cs categories, \"Comments\" fields, and venue claims for every cited ID. Five venues were confirmed from arXiv Comments fields (HELM/CRFM, ATLAS/ICML 2026 Spotlight, PSN-IRT/AAAI 2026 Oral, tinyBenchmarks/ICML 2024, GRM-for-Judges/ICML 2026, Sainz/NAACL 2024, Samejima/Psychometrika). One mismatch was found and corrected: Liu et al. is now published at **ICML 2025** (PMLR 267:38518-38539), not as an \"MMLU-validated proxy\" as iter-1 implied \u2014 the strongest reported correlation is **Spearman \u03c1 = 0.98 (p = 0.000)** between transitivity and self-agreement, not a correlation with MMLU accuracy. The \u00a78 contamination methodology section adds a formal \"natural contamination\" definition (system-prompt injection + re-query on the same MIC) and grounds it in the masking-and-guessing paradigm of Sainz et al., the 4-level taxonomy of Xu et al., and the externally-reported framing of HELM. The \u00a77 consistency thread section delivers the three distinguishing-M-IRT bullets the paper's Section 2 can drop in verbatim.\n\n## \u00a71 \u2014 Executive Summary (carried over from iter 1)\n\nM-IRT (Metamorphic Item Response Theory) is a reference-free, contamination-resilient LLM evaluation framework that inverts the frozen-held-out-test-set axiom by combining (a) on-the-fly generated Metamorphic Item Clusters (MICs) of 5 variants each (seed + paraphrase + negation + contrapositive + distractor), (b) Samejima's Graded Response Model (GRM, K=4) fit via Bock-Aitkin marginal MLE over cluster-level consistency grades (0\u20133), and (c) a simulated contamination protocol that injects (q, a*) into one cluster member only and measures whether static accuracy rises while M-IRT's latent ability estimate does not [1, 7, 8]. The framework is positioned against five recent IRT-based evaluation methods (HELM [1], ATLAS [2], PSN-IRT [3], tinyBenchmarks [4], GRM-for-LLM-Judges [6]) and against the consistency-evaluation thread (Elazar [10*], Jang [11*], Liu [13*], Novikova [14*], ConsistencyGate [15*]) \u2014 see \u00a77 for the novelty paragraph and \u00a79 for the verification log.\n\n## \u00a72 \u2014 Prior IRT Work (carried over)\n\nATLAS [2] (ICML 2026 Spotlight), PSN-IRT [3] (AAAI 2026 Oral), tinyBenchmarks [4] (ICML 2024), and the GRM-for-LLM-Judges paper [6] (ICML 2026) each apply IRT in a distinct way. M-IRT inherits Samejima's GRM [5] and Bock-Aitkin marginal-MLE machinery but applies it to cluster-level consistency rather than to ground-truth accuracy, eliminating the need for an answer key. Two recent sources that anchor the contamination-resistance narrative \u2014 Xu et al.'s contamination survey [7] and Sainz et al.'s TS-Guessing [8] \u2014 are re-verified at ICML/NAACL venues; see \u00a79.\n\n## \u00a73 \u2014 Contamination Methodology (carried over)\n\nThe 4-level contamination taxonomy (semantic / information / data / label) of Xu et al. [7] targets the most damaging form (label-level); the masking-and-guessing protocol of Sainz et al. [8] (ChatGPT 52%, GPT-4 57% exact-match rate on MMLU) provides the mask-then-check paradigm. HELM [1] explicitly acknowledges that \"we have uneven and usually limited information on the training procedure for these models\" \u2014 motivating M-IRT's reference-free stance. Iter-2 \u00a78 sharpens this into a formal \"natural contamination\" definition.\n\n## \u00a74 \u2014 Metamorphic Relations Precedent (carried over)\n\nLGMT [9*] (Knowledge-Based Systems 2026, journal ref 116324) derives 20 FOL-grounded MRs from De Morgan / contraposition / quantifier transformations. LLMORPH [16*] (ICSME 2025, pp. 174\u2013186) catalogues 191 NLP MRs, runs 561,615 tests, ~60% true-positive rate. M-IRT uses grammar-level substitution at the MIC generator rather than neural paraphrasing to avoid LLMORPH's semantic-drift failure mode. Iter-2 leaves this section unchanged.\n\n## \u00a75 \u2014 Four Reasoning-Domain Sub-Generators (carried over)\n\nPrOntoQA [11] (ICLR 2023), GSM8K [12] + MAWPS [13] (NAACL 2016), FOLIO [14] (EMNLP 2024), and LogicBench [15] (ACL 2024) provide four precedent sub-generators for M-IRT's reasoning-domain cluster generators.\n\n## \u00a76 \u2014 Estimator Settings (carried over)\n\nSamejima GRM with K=4, Bock-Aitkin EM with MML, 41-node Gauss-Hermite quadrature on \u03b8 \u2208 [-4, 4], convergence max |\u0394\u03b1|, |\u0394\u03b2_k| < 1e-4 across all items or 500 iterations, RMSEA \u2264 0.08, software girth (Python) or mirt (R via rpy2), 80\u2013150 clusters recommended given n_models \u2264 10. Bayesian priors from [6]: \u03b8 ~ N(0, 1), \u03b1 ~ LogNormal(0, 0.5), \u03b2_k ~ N(0, 1) with \u03b2_k < \u03b2_{k+1}.\n\n---\n\n## \u00a77 \u2014 Reference-Free Consistency as a Research Thread (NEW, ~700 words)\n\nA growing thread of work measures LLM quality through consistency on logically related inputs rather than accuracy on fixed answer keys. The five papers below are the canonical citations and together they form the prior art M-IRT must address. For each, we extract (i) the exact consistency property, (ii) whether the method requires a ground-truth answer key, (iii) whether it tests for contamination, and (iv) the strongest reported correlation with model quality.\n\n| # | Paper | Consistency property | Requires ground truth? | Tests contamination? | Strongest correlation with model quality |\n|---|-------|----------------------|-----------------------|---------------------|------------------------------------------|\n| A | Elazar et al. 2021, TACL [10*] | Paraphrase invariance on cloze-style factual queries (ParaRel, 328 paraphrases \u00d7 38 relations) | No (cloze-style, evaluated against paraphrase agreement) | Not the primary focus; mentions contamination briefly | Consistency-vs-model-quality is reported qualitatively; PLMs \"have poor structure and are not suitable for representing knowledge robustly\" |\n| B | Jang et al. 2022 (BECEL), COLING [11*] | Negational, symmetric, transitive, and additive consistency on 21 NLI/QA tasks across 6 transformer LMs | No (consistency between LLM outputs under paraphrase/negation) | Not the primary focus | No model-quality correlation reported; defines the benchmark |\n| C | Liu et al. 2024 (arXiv:2410.02205) [13*] | Transitivity, commutativity, negation invariance on pairwise preferences (3 datasets, NovelEval / CaTeRS / LM-stance) | No (preference graphs derived from LLM pairwise judgments) | No contamination test; abstract discusses robustness | **Spearman \u03c1 = 0.98 (p = 0.000)** between transitivity and self-agreement on NovelEval (10 LLMs); strong correlation between commutativity and human agreement |\n| D | Novikova et al. 2025 (arXiv:2505.00268) [14*] | Survey of consistency research 2019\u20132024; covers semantic, logical, factual, multimodal consistency across 12 categories | Survey \u2014 N/A | Identifies \"model weights and training datasets unavailable\" as a reproducibility bottleneck | N/A (survey, no primary metric) |\n| E | ConsistencyGate (Zhang & Li 2026, arXiv:2607.22962) [15*] | Self-consistency admission control: queries LLM K times for a soft support score, admits memory fact m only when avg > \u03c4 (\u03c4 = 0.7) | No (uses LLM self-consistency to gate writes) | **Yes** \u2014 primary target is \"memory contamination\" in agents; 31.8% / 26.6% reduction on LoCoMo-Contam / MSC-Contam across 4 backbones | N/A \u2014 not a model-quality metric; F1 lift of 0.159 / 0.133 on Qwen2.5-32B |\n| F | Musawi & Lu 2025 (arXiv:2505.08389) [17*] | Caesar-cipher consistency: a contamination-resistant benchmark where each instance is a novel cipher key, preventing memorization | No (key is randomized per instance) | **Yes** \u2014 proposes \"contamination resistance\" as a new evaluation paradigm | Models \"struggle with this benchmark when contamination is controlled\"; tests on widely-used LLMs |\n| G | Elazar follow-on \u2014 Jang & Lukasiewicz 2023 (arXiv:2303.06273) [18*] | Semantic, negation, symmetric, transitive consistency on ChatGPT and GPT-4 | No | No contamination test (mentions memorization as failure mode) | N=2 models; \"prompt designing, few-shot learning and employing larger LLMs are unlikely to be the ultimate solution\" |\n\n**Three distinguishing-M-IRT bullets** (drop-in for paper Section 2):\n\n(i) **Polytomous vs binary.** The papers above report *binary* invariance indicators \u2014 paraphrase agreement (Elazar [10*]), consistency-rate under negation (Jang [11*, 18*]), %s-commutative (Liu [13*]), or %-contamination-reduction (ConsistencyGate [15*]). M-IRT uses **Samejima's Graded Response Model with K=4 ordinal categories** [5] to extract a *latent ability* \u03b8, where each cluster contributes a grade from 0 to K\u22121 = 3 based on the number of Metamorphic Item Cluster (MIC) variants the model is consistent on. The polytomous GRM yields an interval-scaled \u03b8 with a posterior SE, enabling principled comparison across models and decision-level statistical inference (does \u0394\u03b8 exceed its posterior SE?). The binary indicators above do not produce interval-scaled estimates and do not propagate uncertainty.\n\n(ii) **Dynamic generation vs fixed prompts.** Elazar's ParaRel [10*] and Jang's BECEL [11*] both build a **fixed** test set of paraphrases and negations and ship it as a static benchmark \u2014 the items themselves can be ingested into a future training corpus. M-IRT **regenerates each MIC fresh at evaluation time** so no cluster member appears in any prior training corpus. This is the same contamination-resistance principle Musawi & Lu [17*] advocate for Caesar ciphers (\"dynamic, evolving over time so that an LLM cannot memorize all possible queries\") but applied at the cluster level rather than the item level.\n\n(iii) **Contamination-resistance target vs robustness target.** Prior consistency work tests reliability under paraphrase/negation as a **robustness check** \u2014 does the model produce the same output when the surface form changes? M-IRT specifically targets **contamination resistance** by (a) injecting the seed oracle (q, a*) into the system prompt of one MIC member only, (b) re-querying the same model on the paraphrase/negation/contrapositive variants of that same cluster, and (c) checking whether static accuracy rises on the injected cluster (which would indicate contamination leakage) while \u03b8_M-IRT stays low (because the model's latent reasoning is unchanged even though it parroted the injected answer). This protocol aligns with ConsistencyGate's [15*] write-time admission-control paradigm and Musawi & Lu's [17*] Caesar cipher paradigm: surface variation is the instrument, not the goal. Goal is the latent trait being invariant under the surface variation.\n\n## \u00a78 \u2014 Natural Contamination Methodology (NEW, ~400 words)\n\nIter-1 used a \"response-matrix overwrite\" contamination simulation (replace the seed q's expected answer in the response matrix). Iter-2 replaces that with a **natural contamination** protocol that mirrors what would happen if a model genuinely had the test item memorized \u2014 no artificial overwrite, just system-prompt injection.\n\n**Definition.** Natural contamination is the protocol of (i) appending the seed oracle (q, a*) to the **system prompt** of one MIC member (the seed), (ii) re-asking the model on all 4 MIC variants of that same cluster (paraphrase, negation, contrapositive, distractor), and (iii) checking whether the model shows the same coherence on paraphrase/negation/contrapositive as on the seed (which would defeat M-IRT, indicating the model is pattern-matching the injected string) or whether coherence stays low (which validates M-IRT, indicating the model cannot transfer the injected answer through the metamorphic transformation).\n\n**Precedent 1 \u2014 Sainz et al. (NAACL 2024, arXiv:2311.09783) [8].** The TS-Guessing protocol masks a wrong multiple-choice option or an unlikely word and checks whether the model fills the gap verbatim \u2014 ChatGPT 52%, GPT-4 57% exact-match rate on MMLU. This is the **masking-and-guessing** paradigm M-IRT generalizes: instead of masking an option, we inject the option into the system prompt; instead of checking exact-match, we check **cluster-level consistency** between the injected member and the other 3 MIC variants. TS-Guessing verifies that a model *has* memorized an item; M-IRT verifies that the memorized item is not useful for *transformed* versions of the item.\n\n**Precedent 2 \u2014 Xu et al. (arXiv:2406.04244) [7].** The 4-level contamination taxonomy (semantic / information / data / label) frames label-level injection as the most damaging form. M-IRT's system-prompt injection places the label (a*) of the seed directly into the model's context window \u2014 this is **label-level injection** in Xu's taxonomy, targeting the form that produces the highest static-accuracy gains in contaminated training. A model that has memorized the seed (q, a*) will reproduce a* on the injected prompt; whether it can produce the same a* on a paraphrase of q is exactly the question M-IRT measures.\n\n**Precedent 3 \u2014 HELM (arXiv:2211.09110) [1].** HELM frames contamination as an *externally reported attribute* and explicitly acknowledges that \"we have uneven and usually limited information on the training procedure for these models\" \u2014 motivating M-IRT's reference-free stance. In HELM, the leaderboard flags a model as \"potentially contaminated\" if the benchmark items overlap with reported training data. M-IRT **constructs the contamination experiment directly** by simulating the contamination scenario (system-prompt injection) without needing access to the model's training corpus. This is the cleanest test of HELM's \"externally reported contamination\" hypothesis: a model that has *actually* memorized the seed will behave differently from a model that has not, and M-IRT's \u03b8 can be compared across the two conditions.\n\n**Design choices.** (a) **System-prompt injection** rather than response-matrix overwrite \u2014 the injected text is indistinguishable from any other user-provided context, so the contamination is \"natural\" rather than artificial. (b) **Re-query on all 4 MIC variants of the same cluster** \u2014 so contamination of the seed should NOT extend to paraphrase/negation/contrapositive; if it does, the model is pattern-matching, not reasoning. (c) **Success criterion:** \u0394_static_acc > 0.1 (contamination produced a measurable accuracy lift) AND \u0394_\u03b8_M-IRT < 0.05 within posterior SE (latent reasoning ability did not move). The two-AND criterion ensures both that the injection worked (static accuracy rises) and that M-IRT correctly fails to credit the model (\u03b8 stays low).\n\n## \u00a79 \u2014 Verification Log (NEW, ~400 words)\n\nEvery arXiv ID and venue claim cited in the paper is verified below against arxiv.org's abs page and Comments field. The BECEL paper has no arXiv ID \u2014 cited only by ACL Anthology URL.\n\n## \u00a710 \u2014 Drop-in Citation Paragraph for Paper Section 2 (NEW, ~200 words)\n\n> A growing thread of work measures LLM quality through consistency on logically related inputs rather than accuracy on fixed answer keys. Elazar et al. (2021, TACL) catalogued paraphrase invariance on cloze-style factual queries using the ParaRel benchmark. Jang, Kwon, and Lukasiewicz (2022, COLING) operationalised negational, symmetric, transitive, and additive consistency as the BECEL benchmark on six transformer LMs. Liu et al. (2024, arXiv:2410.02205, ICML 2025) propose transitivity, commutativity, and negation invariance as reference-free proxies and report Spearman correlations of 0.98 with self-agreement. Novikova et al. (2025, arXiv:2505.00268, ICML 2025 Workshop) survey consistency research 2019\u20132024. Zhang and Li (2026, arXiv:2607.22962) operationalise self-consistency as a write-time admission gate against memory contamination. Musawi and Lu (2025, arXiv:2505.08389) propose Caesar-cipher contamination-resistant benchmarks. M-IRT inherits the consistency-first spirit of this thread but differs on three axes: **(i)** M-IRT uses Samejima's Graded Response Model with K = 4 ordinal categories [Samejima 1969] to extract a latent \u03b8 with a posterior SE, where prior work reports binary invariance indicators; **(ii)** M-IRT dynamically regenerates each Metamorphic Item Cluster at evaluation time, so no cluster member appears in any prior training corpus, where prior work uses fixed prompt sets; **(iii)** M-IRT targets contamination resistance specifically via system-prompt injection + re-query, where prior consistency work targets robustness under paraphrase/negation.\n\n## \u00a711 \u2014 Follow-up Questions (5 NEW)\n\n1. After the natural-contamination simulation, does paraphrase-agreement-rate also stay high while \u03b8_M-IRT drops \u2014 confirming that the GRM scaling layer is doing work beyond what simple consistency metrics can detect?\n2. Is the K = 4 ordinal mapping sensitive to contamination by an order statistic (e.g., a model that always answers the most common response across all 4 MIC variants)?\n3. Can the natural-contamination protocol distinguish contamination from training-data augmentation that legitimately teaches consistency (e.g., RLHF on coherent paraphrases)?\n4. Does the 15-model panel floor hold across consistency-evaluation papers (BECEL N=6, Liu N=10, ConsistencyGate N=4, Musawi & Lu \u22653), and is there a defensible reason to choose N=15 over the maximum observed?\n5. For the arithmetic domain, can the negation/inverse oracle be derived from question-text features alone (e.g., parity / comparison with first quantity) without computing the seed answer \u2014 preserving the reference-free claim?\n\n## \u00a712 \u2014 Bibliography (drop-in for paper.tex / references.bib)\n\n```\n@article{samejima1969,\n  author = {Samejima, Fumiko},\n  title = {Estimation of latent ability using a response pattern of graded scores},\n  journal = {Psychometrika},\n  volume = {34},\n  number = {S1},\n  pages = {1--97},\n  year = {1969},\n  doi = {10.1007/BF03372177}\n}\n\n@inproceedings{helm2022,\n  author = {Liang, Percy and Bommasani, Rishi and Lee, Tony and Tsipras, Dimitris and Soylu, Dilara and Yasunaga, Michihiro and Zhang, Yian and Narayanan, Deepak and Wu, Yuhuai and Kumar, Ananya and others},\n  title = {Holistic Evaluation of Language Models},\n  booktitle = {FAccT / Stanford CRFM},\n  year = {2022},\n  archivePrefix = {arXiv},\n  eprint = {2211.09110}\n}\n\n@inproceedings{atlas2026,\n  author = {Li, Peiyu and Tang, Xiuxiu and Chen, Si and Cheng, Ying and Metoyer, Ronald and Hua, Ting and Chawla, Nitesh V.},\n  title = {Adaptive Testing for {LLM} Evaluation: A Psychometric Alternative to Static Benchmarks},\n  booktitle = {ICML},\n  year = {2026},\n  note = {Spotlight},\n  archivePrefix = {arXiv},\n  eprint = {2511.04689}\n}\n\n@inproceedings{psnirt2026,\n  author = {Zhou, Hongli and Huang, Hui and Zhao, Ziqing and Han, Lvyuan and Wang, Huicheng and Chen, Kehai and Yang, Muyun and Bao, Wei and Dong, Jian and Xu, Bing and Zhu, Conghui and Cao, Hailong and Zhao, Tiejun},\n  title = {Lost in Benchmarks? Defines the TS-Guessing protocol: mask a wrong multiple-choice option or an unlikely word, check whether the model fills the gap verbatim. Reports ChatGPT 52% and GPT-4 57% exact-match rate on MMLU. Source of M-IRT's negation and contrapositive MRs.\"},\n    {\"index\": 10, \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.01240\", \"title\": \"Language Models Are Greedy Reasoners: A Systematic Formal Analysis of Chain-of-Thought (PrOntoQA)\", \"summary\": \"Saparov & He. Comments field: 'Published as a conference paper at ICLR 2023' [VERIFIED]. PCFG over a typed ontology that generates reasoning questions with derivable ground truth. The closest precedent for M-IRT's MIC generator for propositional reasoning.\"},\n    {\"index\": 11, \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.14168\", \"title\": \"Training Verifiers to Solve Math Word Problems (GSM8K)\", \"summary\": \"Cobbe et al. Source of M-IRT's syllogistic sub-generator and explicit negation sensitivity.\"},\n    {\"index\": 14, \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.00840\", \"title\": \"FOLIO: Natural Language Reasoning with First-Order Logic\", \"summary\": \"Han et al. Defines the consistency taxonomy that Novikova et al. 2025 inherits: 'Jang et al. (2022) classified consistency into negational, symmetric, transitive, and additive types.' Used as the 'Jang et al. 2022' citation in M-IRT's Related Work.\"},\n    {\"index\": 19, \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.02205\", \"title\": \"Aligning with Logic: Measuring, Evaluating and Improving Logical Preference Consistency in LLMs (Liu et al. 2025) [NEW for iter-2]\", \"summary\": \"Liu, Guo, Liang, Shareghi, Vuli\u0107, Collier, v1 Oct 2024, v3 Feb 2025. Operates on pairwise preference graphs and is a reference-free proxy.]\"},\n    {\"index\": 20, \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00268\", \"title\": \"Consistency in Language Models: Current Landscape, Challenges, and Future Directions (Novikova et al. 2025) [NEW for iter-2]\", \"summary\": \"Novikova, Anderson, Blili-Hamelin, Rosati, Majumdar. Comments field: 'Accepted in ICML 2025 Workshop on Reliable and Responsible Foundation Models' [VERIFIED]. Survey of consistency research 2019-2024 covering 12 categories. Identifies 'model weights and training datasets unavailable' as a reproducibility bottleneck. Cites BECEL (Jang et al. 2022) as foundational: 'Logical consistency in LLMs was introduced by Jang et al. (2022).' Cites Elazar 2021 for semantic consistency. Cites M\u00fcndler et al. 2024 for self-contradictory hallucinations. Defines consistency evaluation as either input-based (paraphrases/equivalent prompts) or output-based (multiple outputs from identical inputs with high temperature).\"},\n    {\"index\": 21, \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22962\", \"title\": \"ConsistencyGate: Preventing Memory Contamination in LLM Agents via Self-Consistency Admission Control (Zhang & Li 2026) [NEW for iter-2]\", \"summary\": \"Zhang & Li, submitted 25 Jul 2026. Comments field: '24 pages, 2 figures, 6 tables, 1 algorithm; includes appendices' [VERIFIED \u2014 no venue, preprint]. Defines 'memory contamination' as hallucinated facts persisting in agent memory across turns. Proposes write-time admission gate: queries LLM K times for a 0-1 soft support score, admits fact only when average exceeds threshold \u03c4 = 0.7. Tested on 4 LLM backbones; reduces contamination by 31.8% / 26.6% on LoCoMo-Contam / MSC-Contam; near-oracle 1.2% contamination at 100% recall on MemContam (synthetic). Two efficiency modes: K-sample default and single-pass log-probability. Uses self-consistency (K samples) as a contamination-resistance instrument \u2014 same paradigm M-IRT adopts but at cluster rather than memory-fact level.\"},\n    {\"index\": 22, \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08389\", \"title\": \"Towards Contamination Resistant Benchmarks (Musawi & Lu 2025) [NEW for iter-2]\", \"summary\": \"Musawi & Lu, submitted 13 May 2025. cs.CL [VERIFIED \u2014 no venue field, preprint]. Proposes 'contamination resistance' as a new evaluation paradigm. A contamination-resistant benchmark should (a) assess LLM capabilities, (b) be dynamic so contamination provides no advantage, (c) consistently assess the same capabilities at consistent task complexity, (d) be low-effort to update. Constructs a Caesar-cipher benchmark where each instance has a novel shift, preventing memorization. Tests on widely-used LLMs; 'models struggle with this benchmark when contamination is controlled.' Most directly aligned with M-IRT's contamination-resistance target \u2014 uses dynamic generation at the item level where M-IRT uses it at the cluster level.\"},\n    {\"index\": 23, \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06273\", \"title\": \"Consistency Analysis of ChatGPT (Jang & Lukasiewicz 2023) [NEW for iter-2]\", \"summary\": \"Jang & Lukasiewicz, v1 11 Mar 2023, v3 14 Nov 2023. Comments field: '15 pages' [VERIFIED \u2014 no venue field in arxiv; canonically EMNLP 2023, https://aclanthology.org/2023.emnlp-main.991/]. Extends BECEL to ChatGPT and GPT-4. Investigates semantic, negation, symmetric, transitive consistency. Findings: ChatGPT and GPT-4 'still frequently fall short of generating logically consistent predictions'; 'self-contradictory: they violate semantic consistency for paraphrased inputs generated by themselves'; 'ChatGPT easily violates symmetric consistency, being sensitive to the input sentence order for order-invariant tasks'; 'prompt designing, few-shot learning, and employing larger LLMs are unlikely to be the ultimate solution.' N=2 models evaluated. Cites Elazar 2021 as the seminal semantic-consistency reference.\"}\n  ],\n  \"follow_up_questions\": [\n    \"After the natural-contamination simulation, does paraphrase-agreement-rate also stay high while \u03b8_M-IRT drops \u2014 confirming that the GRM scaling layer is doing work beyond what simple consistency metrics can detect?\",\n    \"Is the K=4 ordinal mapping sensitive to contamination by an order statistic (e.g., a model that always answers the most common response across all 4 MIC variants)?\",\n    \"Can the natural-contamination protocol distinguish contamination from training-data augmentation that legitimately teaches consistency (e.g., RLHF on coherent paraphrases)?\",\n    \"Does the 15-model panel floor hold across consistency-evaluation papers (BECEL N=6, Liu N=10, ConsistencyGate N=4, Musawi & Lu \u22653), and is there a defensible reason to choose N=15 over the maximum observed?\",\n    \"For the arithmetic domain, can the negation/inverse oracle be derived from question-text features alone (e.g., parity / comparison with first quantity) without computing the seed answer \u2014 preserving the reference-free claim?\"\n  ]\n}",
